|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF2 monomer, 59 res | 0.49 s | 1.30 s | 0.21 s |
| AF2 multimer, 59 res | 0.51 | 1.23 | 0.22 |
| AF2, 400 res x 512 rows x 1024 extra | | 4.16 | 3.44 |
| ESMFold2, 40 res (int5) | 0.35 | 2.87 | 0.88 |
| AF3, 68 tokens, 200 steps | | 5.2 | 3.07 |
| OpenDDE 6MRR, 200 steps | 1.73 | 6.75 | 4.08 |
| OpenDDE, 200 res / 384 structural tokens, 50 steps | | 8.48 | 5.99 |

    tools/fold-in-page.py, a fresh profile each time, the page's own timer:
    monomer 2.0 s   multimer 1.9 s   af3 3 s   ef2-fast-600m 2.9 s   opendde 5 s

## AF2's triangle multiplication and outer product mean, on the device

The two packers the int8 commit left on the host, and the last two AF2 had.

🔴 **THE MATRIX LAYOUT'S INTERLEAVE IS A TRANSPOSE, AND IT CANCELS THE
LOADER'S.** `interleaveAB` reads `source[h * cZ + c]` out of an array the
fixture already transposed from the stored `[c][h]`, and writes it at
`c * 4cH + 4h + role`. Composed, destination element `d` of role `d % 4` is
element `d / 4` of that role's RAW tensor - four contiguous parts alternating
element by element, `partRun` 1, no stride at all. `transposeZG` cancels the
same way, so both output matrices are the raw tensor too. What looked like the
riskiest conversion left is the simplest one in the file.

    an order entry may now name up to four sources and state its own map, and
    the entry's map WINS over the source's - a pack that cancels a transpose
    would otherwise get a correctly shaped, entirely wrong matrix. A multi-part
    entry that states no map is a refusal, not a guess.

The outer product mean needed no mapping at all: eight tensors, none reshaped,
so its device pack is `OUTER_PRODUCT_MEAN_PACK_ORDER` and nothing else.

| | load | first fold |
|---|---:|---:|
| monomer, before | 497 ms | 1275 ms |
| after the triangle | 375 | 1185 |
| after the outer product mean | 377 | 1166 |
| multimer, before | 505 | 1230 |
| after both | 374 | 1085 |

`packBy` is now empty on both: not one AF2 weight buffer is built on the host.

## The first fold WAS the compile queue: 95 pipelines, 1.47 ever in flight

With the packing gone, `tools/gpu/probe-af2-warmup.js` asked what the other 942
ms of a 1163 ms first fold was, and the answer was one number:

    pipelinesAsync 95     pipelineSpanMs 1133.1     pipelineAwaitSumMs 1662.4
    shaderModuleMs 3.5    writeBufferMs 65.9        bufferMs 9.8

🔴 **THE SPAN FROM THE FIRST `createComputePipelineAsync` TO THE LAST SETTLE WAS
THE WHOLE FOLD.** An encode asks for a pipeline at the moment it first needs it,
and a block's ten operations run one after another, so an average of 1.47 were
ever in flight. This browser compiles 32 pipelines **5.4x** faster concurrently
than serially - 110 ms against 593 - so the fix is to ask for all of them first.

`Execution.warm` is a MODE, not a second list of shader keys, which is the whole
point: an enumeration of kernels written beside the encode path is a list
written twice, and the copy that goes stale is the one nobody runs. Every encode
function returns as soon as its pipelines are requested; `staged` hands its
promise to the execution instead of awaiting it; the model warms block 0 of each
stack before either runs. Block 0 is every key both stacks use - blocks differ
in their weights, and a pipeline key is shapes.

| | first fold | repeat |
|---|---:|---:|
| monomer before | 1163 ms | 215 |
| monomer after | **992** | 215 |
| multimer after | **939** | 218 |

**And warming EARLIER, so it overlaps the template embedder, is worth nothing.**
Started before the template and awaited after it, the fold was 991 ms against
992 - because the template's own compiles and the warm's contend for the same
compiler threads. The pool is the constraint, not the placement.

## One staging array instead of 474

`blockUploadStats` says what the on-device decode costs the HOST, which no
profiler here could see - it is not inside a compute pass:

    calls 474   tensors 4292   submits 474   buffers 1896
    stagingBytes 96.5 MB   stagingMs 129.2   totalMs 209.6

🔴 **713 MB/s FOR A MEMCPY MEANS THE COPY IS NOT THE COST.** `runBlockUpload`
allocated a fresh `new Uint8Array` per tensor set - 1422 of them a fold, each
allocated and zero-filled by the engine before a byte was copied into it. One
shared scratch that grows and never shrinks takes the assembly to **61.1 ms**
and the whole decode to **139**.

It is safe because nothing reads a chunk's padding: positions are rounded up to
four bytes and a decode reads at most one byte past its last group - the slack
byte, which `codeSpan` puts INSIDE `byteLength`. The one hazard was the ZEROS
buffer of a symmetric codec, which is bound and never read and used to be zero
by accident of being freshly allocated; an empty chunk list is skipped entirely
now, so it keeps WebGPU's own zero-initialisation.

| | first fold |
|---|---:|
| monomer | 992 -> **851 ms** |
| multimer | 939 -> **835** |
| in the page | 1.5 -> **1.3 s** |

## Where compilation is a bottleneck, and where it is not

`tools/gpu/probe-compiles.js --tool=<any tool>` wraps another tool's `main`
rather than being one, so every fold gate can be measured without ceasing to be
a gate. Its number is **`busyMs`** - the union of the intervals, the time with
at least one compile in flight - and NOT the sum, because a stage that compiles
twenty at once and a stage that compiles them one at a time have the same sum
and nothing else in common.

| model | wall | compile busy | while busy |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF2 monomer (after the warm) | 1245 ms | 455 | 9.5 in flight |
| ESMFold2 int5 | 3491 | ~470 | - |
| AF3, 68 tokens | 4387 | **840** | 13.9 |
| OpenDDE 6MRR | 8226 | **1253** | 13.7 |

🔴 **SO AF3 AND OPENDDE HAD ALREADY SOLVED THIS AND AF2 HAD NOT.**
`Af3PairformerStackGpu.#runStack` collects every `pipelines.get` into one array
and awaits it once, and the per-group numbers show it working: `af3-msa` is 38
pipelines summing 2507 ms in 129 ms of busy, `af3-template` 29 summing 1648 in
100, `af3-block` 111 summing 9123 in 455. Their long spans are stages running at
different TIMES, not compiles running one at a time - which is exactly the
distinction `sumMs / spanMs` cannot make and `busyMs` can. A first fold of
either is weight I/O and sampler steps, not shader compilation, and the warm
that paid 171 ms on AF2 has nothing to collect there.

## ESMFold2's trunk was the last caller packing a pair track on the host

`packPairTrackWeights` has four callers - AF3's pairformer, its MSA stack, its
template embedder and ESMFold2's trunk - and three of them had been decoding on
the device since `src/af3/pair-track-device-weights.js` was written. The fourth
had not, and it showed as one number: **`trunk 0` 828 ms against 32 for the same
block warm**, inside a 2.70 s first fold.

Wiring it up is four lines. It then did nothing at all, twice, and the reason is
worth the entry:

🔴 **THREE MODULES DECLARED THEIR OWN `SOURCES` SYMBOL.**
`src/reference/alphafold-fixture.js`, `src/af3/weights.js` and
`src/esmfold2/weights.js` each had `export const SOURCES = Symbol(...)`, and a
symbol is unique by IDENTITY and not by description - which is the entire point
of one, and exactly what makes two of them indistinguishable at a lookup.
`weights[af3.SOURCES]` on an object carrying `esmfold2.SOURCES` is not a
refusal, it is `undefined`, and `undefined` here means "this bundle cannot
supply codes, pack it on the host". There is one symbol now, in
`src/runtime/weight-sources.js`, and the three re-export it.

🔴 **AND THEN THE ENTRY SHAPES DIFFERED.** AF3's SOURCES values are thunks -
functions with `store`, `tensorName`, `first` and `count` hung off them -
and ESMFold2's are the source records. The decoder never calls either; it reads
those four fields. So `bindable()` is one predicate that accepts both, beside
the symbol, and `typeof entry === "function"` is gone from three call sites.

| | first fold | `trunk 0` | repeat |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| before | 2.70 s | 828 ms | 0.88 s |
| after | **2.25** | **360** | 0.88 |
| in the page | 2.7 s -> **2.2** | | |

`tools/gpu/check-esmfold2-trunk-pack.js` runs the trunk both ways over one pair
in one process and holds them to **zero differing elements** - not a tolerance,
because the decoder is bit-identical and the destination layout is the same, so
any difference is a mapping. It also asserts the device arm actually decoded:
two host arms would agree perfectly.

## A measurement that inverted: `mappedAtCreation` for the staging

The staging assembly is two copies of every byte - gather the chunks into a
JavaScript array, then `writeBuffer` that into the driver's. Measured on an
ESMFold2 fold that is 209 ms of assembly and **227 ms of writeBuffer over 426
MiB**, and `mappedAtCreation` removes the second copy by gathering straight into
the mapped range. It also removes the need for `COPY_DST` and for a staging
pool, and the mapped range is zero-initialised, which is a nicer answer to the
"a symmetric codec's unread zeros buffer must stay zero" problem than skipping
the assembly.

It is **35% slower**, twice measured:

| | assembly | writeBuffer | createBuffer | ESM-C tower | fold |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| a JS scratch, then writeBuffer | 209 ms | 227 | 8.8 | 989 ms | **2.23 s** |
| `mappedAtCreation` | 144 | 52.8 | **118.7** | 1350 | 3.01 |

Both halves of the copy did get cheaper and the buffer creation did not: a
`mappedAtCreation` STORAGE buffer has to be allocated somewhere the host can
write, which is not where a storage buffer wants to live, and Dawn pays for that
on the unmap this measurement does not see. Reverted. **The two-copy path is the
fast one here.**

## A pooled staging buffer is worth nothing, measured

Between those two, the staging GPU buffers were pooled per device rather than
created per upload - 1224 `createBuffer`/`destroy` pairs a fold looked like an
obvious cost. It moved the ESMFold2 decode from 602 ms to 612: `createBuffer`
is 8.8 ms of a fold in total. The 400 ms that looked like allocation was
`await uploadPipeline(...)` on an already-built pipeline, once per TENSOR -
event-loop time, not CPU - and caching the RESOLVED pipeline beside its promise
took the decode to 314 ms. That one is kept; the pool is not.

## `prefetch` on the fold tools, and where it is worth anything

`HttpTensorStore.prefetch` schedules every shard instead of letting them arrive
as tensors are asked for. It is opt-in, and its own comment says why: a bench
that reads four blocks should not pull the whole manifest. **What it did not say
is that the whole-model fold TOOLS are not benches.** They are now the same
shape as the page, which has called it since it was written.

| tool | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| `fold-esmfold2.js` (its own reader, the ESM-C tower) | 2.25 s | **1.75** |
| `fold-af2.js` | 379 ms | 361 |
| `fold-opendde.js` | 1.759 s | 1.769 |
| `fold.js` (AF3) | - | unchanged |

🔴 **AND ONLY THE STREAMING ONE MOVED, WHICH IS THE POINT.** AF2, AF3 and
OpenDDE load their stacks through one `Promise.all` over every tensor, so every
shard is already requested in the first tick and there is nothing left to
schedule. The ESM-C tower does not: it streams 36 blocks precisely so that 2190
MiB of float32 is never in the tab at once, and asks for a shard when it reaches
one. Measured inside the tower's own loop, 522 ms of a 934 ms pass was
`await pending` - the NEXT block's weights - which is the download and nothing
else.

## Measured and not kept: a submission window on the ESM-C tower

The tower waits for the device after each of its 36 blocks, to release that
block's scratch. A released buffer here goes back to the allocator's POOL rather
than being destroyed, so a window of eight is safe for the reason AF2's
evoformer stack has always relied on - a later submit reads what an earlier one
wrote. It is also **worth nothing**: the fold is 1.68, 1.67 and 1.70 s at
windows of 1, 8 and 36, the tower pass 428, 438 and 440 ms, a warm repeat's
tower 105, 109 and 106, and the atom checksum is -128226 at all three. The 36
syncs are not what the tower is waiting for; the download was.

`fold-esmfold2.js` keeps the atom checksum that measurement needed, because a
fold tool that prints a pLDDT and a contact count can watch a structure move and
report the same two numbers - the same lesson `meanPlddt` taught on AF3.

## Where the five models stand, and the five page folds

| | load | first fold | warm |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF2 monomer, 59 res | 0.36 s | 0.84 s | 0.21 s |
| AF2 multimer, 59 res | 0.37 | 0.86 | 0.22 |
| AF2, 400 res x 512 rows x 1024 extra | | 4.01 | 3.44 |
| ESMFold2, 40 res (int5) | 0.21 | 1.75 | 0.87 |
| AF3, 68 tokens, 50 steps | | 3.21 | |
| OpenDDE 6MRR, 200 steps | 1.77 | 6.40 | 4.09 |

    tools/fold-in-page.py, a fresh profile each time, the page's own timer:
    monomer 1.4 s   multimer 1.3 s   af3 3 s   ef2-fast-600m 2.2 s   opendde 5 s

At the start of this session those five were 2.5, 2.4, 4, 3.9 and "never folds".

## Two more staging measurements that inverted, and what the gather actually is

The decode's host cost is dominated by one line - gathering a plan's chunks into
a scratch array before `writeBuffer` - and on an OpenDDE fold at the page's
sixteen steps that is **311 ms of a 3.5 s wall for 517 MB**. Two ways round it,
both slower:

| | writeBuffer calls | assembly | writeBuffer | fold |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| gather, then one write (shipped) | 3337 | **311 ms** | **444** | **3.56 s** |
| one write a chunk, straight from the shard | 8905 | 359 | 524 | 3.58 |
| `mappedAtCreation`, gather into the map | - | 144 | 52.8 | 3.01 (ESMFold2 2.23 -> 3.01) |

🔴 **AND ROUNDING A CHUNK'S LENGTH UP TO FOUR IS SOUND, WHICH IS WORTH KEEPING
EVEN THOUGH THE ARM LOST.** `writeBuffer` wants a multiple of four and a code
run is `groups * groupBytes + 1`. Into the destination the rounding is free,
because `at` positions are already spaced by `ceil(byteLength / 4) * 4`; out of
the source it reads the NEXT tensor's codes, which this decode never touches -
it reads at most one byte past its last group and `codeSpan` puts that byte
inside `byteLength`. Only the last tensor in a shard would round past the end of
the ArrayBuffer. The arm folded 6MRR to pLDDT 92.05053095535119, the same as the
gather to sixteen digits, so the mapping is right and the cost is per-call
overhead: **2.7x the writeBuffer calls for 1.15x the writeBuffer time and 1.15x
the assembly it was meant to remove.**

A single-chunk fast path was tried first and never fired: 432 upload calls carry
1952 tensors on this bundle, so almost every plan has several chunks.

## AF3's first fold is 3.16 s and its warm one is 1.20, and the gap is not what the code says

`fold.js --folds=2` and a per-stage clock the tool did not have (`stageMilliseconds`,
added here for the reason `fold-opendde.js` grew one: a caller's clock
attributes the gap between two stages to the EARLIER one, so a fold whose named
stages stop at `trunk-done` hides everything after it).

    fold 1  3.188 s     sample-start 1687   trunk-stage 655   pairformer-block 215
    fold 2  1.206 s     sample-start  730

So **1.96 s of warm-up**, and the first sampler call carries 957 ms of it.
`bench-head.js --calls=4` at 68 tokens says where: call 0 is **1218 ms** against
10 steady, split conditioning 114, atom-encoder 124, transformer **904**,
decoder 52.

🔴 **AND `head.warm` WAS WARMING THE WRONG THING.** `probe-warm.js` at 68 tokens
says the transformer's compile takes **71 ms**. The 904 was never the compile;
it is building 396 MiB of resident block weights, which happens in the first
`run`. So the warm was covering 71 ms of a 904 ms stage and its comment - "the
trunk leaves the host idle, so this is free where it stands" - was about the
half that was already free.

**Warming the weights too is worth nothing, and that is the finding.** Both
arms, sequential and all twenty-four concurrent:

| | trunk-stage | sample-start | fold 1 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| compile only (shipped) | 751 ms | 2417 | 3.245 s |
| ...and the weights, one block at a time | 905 | 2276 | 3.177 |
| ...and the weights, all at once | 898 | 2260 | 3.159 |

🔴 **THE TRUNK IS NOT HOST-IDLE ANY MORE.** 141 ms left the sampler and 154
arrived in the trunk. That comment was written when the trunk uploaded its
weights per pass and waited on the GPU for nearly all of its wall; residency,
the device decode and one compute pass a block have since taken the waiting out,
and what is left is host-bound. **Moving host work around a host-bound fold is
not an optimisation** - only removing it is. Reverted.

Where the 1.96 s actually is, as far as it can be attributed: compile busy
**832 ms** (223 pipelines, 14.55 in flight while busy, so the compiler pool is
saturated and more concurrency would not help), the device weight decode's host
half **230 ms**, and ~900 ms that is none of the things this can count -
`createBindGroup` is 23 ms over 4875 calls and `createBuffer` 18 ms over 3105,
but those 3105 buffers are **2.65 GiB** a first fold allocates and a warm one
takes from the pool.

🔴 **AND THE SHADER SOURCE IS NOT IT.** Generating WGSL is host work on the
critical path that no profiler here can see - it happens in a template literal
before `createShaderModule`. A whole AF3 fold writes **1.4 MiB** of it across
223 modules, and `createShaderModule` itself is 6.6 ms. Ruled out.

## What the HOST packers still cost, which nothing could see

`residentPackStats` in src/runtime/resident.js times the `pack()` thunk behind
every resident weight buffer and keeps a per-label table. It exists because
every device decode on the AF3 side falls back to one of those thunks silently
by design, and **a fallback that costs 400 ms of a first fold looks exactly like
a slow machine**. `fold-af2.js` has reported `packBy` since the same question
was asked there; this is the same accounting one level down, so every model gets
it. `tools/gpu/probe-compiles.js` prints it for whatever tool it wraps.

On an AF3 fold it said **676 ms in 156 calls**, and the table said where:

    difftx.zerogate.resident   1 call    496 ms   81.2 MiB
    w.pair-transition         56 calls    94 ms   42.1 MiB
    w.single-transition        4 calls    54 ms   27.0 MiB
    w.single                   4 calls    23 ms   11.3 MiB
    ...eight more                          9 ms

### The batched zero gate: one call, 496 ms

🔴 **CONCATENATING TWENTY-FOUR BLOCKS' GATE TENSORS DECODES TWENTY-FOUR BLOCKS.**
`packZeroGateWeights` lays twelve tensors from each of the 24 diffusion blocks
end to end - and reading them is exactly the int5 decode the device path exists
to leave undone, on weights `run` then releases again. It is a plain
concatenation, so the decoder takes it whole; what it needed was **an order
entry that can name its own holder**, because this is one buffer over
twenty-four SOURCES maps.

`ZERO_GATE_ORDER` is exported for the reason `TRANSITION_PACK_ORDER` is: the
shader's `ZG_*` offsets are running sums of those lengths, so a list written
twice reads a neighbouring tensor - a wrong fold and not a crash.

### And the pair track's transitions, which were waiting on an f32 destination

🔴 **`residentPackedOnDevice` WROTE HALVES ONLY, SO EVERY CALLER GATED ITSELF ON
f16 - AND AF3's PAIR TRACK IS DELIBERATELY f32.** That one line is why
`w.pair-transition` was still 56 host packs with the pairformer, the MSA stack
and the confidence head all asking for it, and why `w.single-transition` and
`w.single` were four each. `planBlockUpload` has taken an f32 destination since
it was written - an f32 element IS a word, which is what lets it stride at all;
only the wrapper had not.

| | host packing | first fold | in the page |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF3, before | 676 ms | 3.19 s | 3 s |
| ...zero gate on the device | 177 | 2.73 | |
| ...and the transitions | **13** | **2.47** | **2 s** |
| OpenDDE 6MRR, 16 steps | ~650 -> **23** | 3.55 -> **2.93 s** | 5 -> **4 s** |
| ESMFold2, and its trunk's transition | 159 -> **1** | 1.75 -> **1.53 s** | 2.3 -> **2.0 s** |
| AF2 | 0 | 0.91 | 1.4 |

pLDDT 85.83009409489117, 92.05053095535119 and atom checksum -128226 unchanged
throughout; `check-difftx-splits` 151/151, `check-af3-block-any` pair
0.0308058352612606 resident and not, `check-af3-msa-block` 5.3955e-6,
`check-esmfold2-trunk-pack` zero differing elements at three decodes a block.

## Where the five models stand at the end of this session

| | load | first fold | warm |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF2 monomer, 59 res | 0.37 s | 0.86 s | 0.21 s |
| AF2 multimer, 59 res | 0.37 | 0.86 | 0.21 |
| AF2, 400 res x 512 rows x 1024 extra | | 4.01 | 3.44 |
| ESMFold2, 40 res (int5) | 0.21 | 1.53 | 0.86 |
| AF3, 68 tokens, 50 steps | | **2.23** | 1.21 |
| OpenDDE 6MRR, 16 steps (the page's count) | 1.9 | **2.54** | 1.35 |

    tools/fold-in-page.py, a fresh profile each time, the page's own timer:
    monomer 1.3 s   multimer 1.3 s   af3 2 s   ef2-fast-600m 2.0 s   opendde 4 s

At the start of this session: **2.5, 2.4, 4, 3.9 and a fold that never
finished.**

What is left, measured and named: OpenDDE's **1.23 s of shader compilation**
(14 pipelines in flight while busy, so the compiler pool is saturated and more
concurrency will not help) and its **1.74 s of weight download**; AF3's 0.83 s
of the same compilation; and the staging gather, 205-321 ms a fold, which two
attempts to remove made worse.

## And what is still decoded on the host: almost nothing

`tensorDecodeStats` in src/reference/http-tensor-store.js is the counterpart to
`residentPackStats` - that one prices the packers, this one prices the decodes
underneath them and every eager `store.tensor(name)` besides. Both print under
`probe-compiles.js`. After this session:

| | compile busy | host packing | host decode |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| AF2 monomer | 466 ms | 0 | 5 ms, 2.3 M elements |
| AF3, 68 tokens | 795 | 10 ms | 32 ms, 4.3 M |
| ESMFold2 int5 | 316 | 1 | **0** |
| OpenDDE 6MRR, 16 steps | 1235 | 22 | 84 ms, 12.9 M |

🔴 **SO THE WEIGHTS ARE OFF THE HOST, AND WHAT IS LEFT IS THE COMPILER AND THE
WIRE.** OpenDDE's 1.76 s weight load is 1.33 s of download - `probe-shard-read`
says 373 MB/s over its 12 shards, which is this browser's six-connection
ceiling - and ~330 ms of loader bookkeeping that is neither a decode (84 ms) nor
a pack (22). Its 1.23 s of compilation runs at 14 pipelines in flight while
busy, which is the pool saturated; AF3's 0.80 s the same.

Two things follow for anyone continuing:

- **The page already prefetches on model SELECTION** (`web/af3-model.js` and
  `web/esmfold2-model.js` both call `store.prefetch()` there), so a user who
  types a sequence has paid the download before clicking. `fold-in-page.py`
  selects and clicks immediately, which is why its number still carries it.
- **Compilation can only come down by compiling less.** More concurrency does
  not help a saturated pool - measured twice, on AF2 where it did help (1.47 in
  flight before) and on AF3 where it did not (14.55). The pairformer's shaders
  are specialised on the token count, which is why OpenDDE compiles
  `af3-block:68:384:...` and `af3-block:130:384:...` as two whole sets in one
  fold - a deliberate trade against the 4.3x a runtime loop bound costs, and
  the place to look if that ceiling ever has to move.

## The staging gather, asked a third and fourth time

It is the last big host cost - 322 ms of an OpenDDE fold, 219 of an ESMFold2
one, 227 of an AF3 one - and two more attempts on it, both recorded so nobody
spends the afternoon again.

**Copying word at a time instead of byte at a time: 322 -> 304 ms.** Every `at`
is a multiple of four by construction and every chunk starts at
`firstGroup * groupBytes`, whose group is a whole number of words in every
codec, so the bulk of each chunk can go through a `Uint32Array` view with only
the tail byte-wise. 6%. The gather is memory-bandwidth bound and not
byte-wise-bound; `Uint8Array.prototype.set` was already a memmove. Not kept.

**Binding the SHARD instead of a staging buffer, and letting the decoder index
into it - declined, with the number that decides it.** The idea is sound: the
codes are already contiguous in the shard the fetch produced, so a plan could
bind that and skip the gather entirely. What it needs is for a plan's chunks to
live in ONE buffer, and they do not:

    fold-opendde   0 of 445 plans read one shard      (largest shard 40.5 MiB)
    fold           0 of 417
    fold-af2       0 of 474                            (12.1 MiB)
    fold-esmfold2  282 of 378                          (15.3 MiB)

A tensor's codes, scales and zeros share its shard; a PLAN's several tensors do
not, because a stacked tensor is one tensor and a block's sixteen are sixteen.
So it would need a bind group per shard inside the dispatch loop and a staging
buffer beside it for the params that still span two - two paths where there is
one - and it would hold the whole bundle on the device (495 MiB for OpenDDE)
until every weight had been decoded. Recorded rather than built.

## Two things that look like per-dispatch overhead and are not

An AF2 block at 400 residues and 512 rows is 60.4 ms of GPU inside 88 ms of
wall, 160 dispatches, and `profile-af2-block.js` attributes 19.4 ms a block to
encoding. Two obvious suspects in `execution.dispatch`, both measured and both
worth nothing:

- **`pipeline.getBindGroupLayout(0)` on every dispatch.** It mints a new
  GPUBindGroupLayout object each call, so memoising it per pipeline in a
  WeakMap looks free. 19.4 -> 19.3 ms a block, and `bench-af2-warm` at 400
  residues is 3.44 s either way.
- **`pushDebugGroup`/`popDebugGroup` around every dispatch** - two wire commands
  each, 15,360 of them a pass at 48 blocks, and nothing in this repository reads
  them (the AF2 timestamp profiler uses `timestampWrites` and `profile.js` uses
  pass labels). Removing both: the same numbers again.

Reverted. The encode time is somewhere else, and `bindGroupMs` measured on AF3
says where it is not: **22.9 ms over 4875 `createBindGroup` calls**, 4.7
microseconds each.

## The compiler works through the download now: AF3 2.52 s -> 2.23, OpenDDE 4.62 -> 4.46

The last big item on the board was shader compilation - 0.80 s of AF3's fold and
1.23 s of OpenDDE's, at fourteen pipelines in flight while busy, which is the
pool saturated. More concurrency cannot help a saturated pool (measured twice:
it helped AF2 at 1.47 in flight and did nothing for AF3 at 14.55). **Only moving
it earlier can, and in front of every fold there is a weight download the
compiler sleeps through** - 1.9 s for OpenDDE, and the tools' clocks do not even
cover it.

🔴 **NOTHING A SHADER IS GENERATED FROM IS A WEIGHT VALUE.** The widths come
from `store.shape`, and an offset table is a running sum of tensor LENGTHS -
which `stacked` also takes from the shape, before a byte of any shard has
arrived. So `bind(store, fields, { shapesOnly: true })` builds one block of
correctly sized ZEROS out of the manifest alone, and
`Af3PairformerStackGpu.warm` compiles against that.

**Zeros and not `{length}`** is what makes it free of every packer:
`packTransitionWeights(x).offsets` sums lengths and then concatenates, and
handed real arrays it concatenates zeros and the offsets are right. Nothing had
to learn a second way to be measured. The tree is one block's - tens of MiB,
transient.

🔴 **AND A WARM THAT GETS IT WRONG IS WASTED WORK, NEVER A WRONG ANSWER**, which
is what makes this safe to do speculatively: the stack still asks the pipeline
cache for its own keys when it runs. The failure mode is silent waste, so the
gate is a COUNT - `--no-warm` on `fold.js` and `fold-opendde.js`, and the
pipeline total either side:

| | pipelines | note |
|---|---:|---:|
| OpenDDE, no warm | 269 | |
| ...warmed with the trunk's root at both token counts | **322** | 53 wasted: the refiner is its own root |
| ...with the refiner's root | 285 | 16 wasted: f32 triangles and transitions |
| ...and the fold's own precision rule | **269** | zero extra, zero missing |

Those sixteen were `defaultPairWeightPrecision`: OpenDDE's pair track is f16
because it is 384 channels wide and the warm defaulted to f32. The rule is
exported now, so the two cannot disagree.

| | first fold | total with the load |
|---|---:|---:|
| AF3, `--no-warm` | 2.522 s | |
| AF3, warmed | **2.234** | |
| OpenDDE 16 steps, `--no-warm` | 2.857 s | 4.621 s |
| OpenDDE, warmed | **2.539** | **4.458** |

pLDDT 85.83009409489117 and 92.05053095535119 to the last digit either way.

🔴 **AND WARMING IT INSIDE `foldBatch` INSTEAD IS WORTH NOTHING.** With the
weights already loaded there is no stand-in needed and the warm can use the real
block - it fires beside `head.warm`, before the embedder, the template embedder
and the MSA stack have each had their turn at the compiler. AF3's fold is 3637
ms against 3628 with it, and the page's AF3 and OpenDDE both read the same
second. The pool is busy for that whole stretch already; the idle the warm has
to fill is the DOWNLOAD, and only a caller holding the store before the loaders
can reach it. Reverted, and `warmTrunkPipelines` is where a page should call
from.

## The pre-merge sweep, and the one thing it found

Two bugs, both in the warm work above, both found by running the gates rather
than by reading the diff:

🔴 **`residentPackedOnDevice` OUTSIDE THE RESIDENCY GATE KILLS THE FOLD ON ITS
OWN RESTART.** It allocates through `noteAllocation`, which RAISES over a budget
rather than evicting, and `Af3PairformerStackGpu.run` catches exactly one of
those: it drops residency, releases what it held and restarts. A call that
ignores `this.residentWeights` raises a SECOND time out of the restart, and
nothing catches that. `--budget=200`: *"w.single-transition needs 3.4 MiB, which
would take this device to 202.6 MiB"*, out of `#runStack` called from the
`catch`. The old `context.weightPrecision === "f16"` gate hid it, because
neither machine this repository runs on has `shader-f16` under its driver -
taking the gate off to reach the f32 pair track exposed it everywhere. Both
single-track packs are gated on `residentWeights` now, like the pair one.

🔴 **AND A WARM MUST NOT ALLOCATE A PAIR REPRESENTATION TO SATISFY A CHECK IT
DOES NOT NEED.** `#runStack` validates `state.pair.length` before it compiles;
the first `warm` handed it real zeros, which is `n^2 * pairChannels` floats -
**245 MiB at 400 tokens and 1.5 GiB at 1000** - for two comparisons the compile
never reads. Skipped under `compilingOnly` instead.

**And one that is not new.** `tools/gpu/fold.js --budget=200` does not complete,
on this branch or on `main`: main dies at `w.single-transition` and this branch
gets past the pairformer and dies at `difftx.block needs 15.8 MiB`. AF3's
diffusion transformer holds ~400 MiB of weights and a 200 MiB device cannot
stream a block of it alongside the trunk's scratch. It is not one of the
documented small-device gates - those are `bench-trunk.js --budget=200` (green,
with `--model=`, which its default does not have on this box) and
`fold-af2.js --budget=200` (green, peak 117.3 MiB) - but it is worth knowing
which of the two it is before someone reads the failure as a regression.

## Upstream's LayerNorm rearrangement, measured here: 1.3% and not 3.6%

`martin-steinegger/alphafold2-webgpu` rewrote its row LayerNorm in September
2026 and records 1.6x-3.6x on the kernel, normalization falling from 11.5% of a
main Evoformer block to 5.5% at 825 residues, and **3.6% of the trunk**. Ours is
character-for-character the arrangement they replaced - one row a workgroup of
64, reduced twice through a halving tree in workgroup memory, six barriers a
reduction - in `src/evoformer/attention.js`, `transition.js`,
`outer-product-mean.js` and `src/structure/iteration.js`. So the question was
whether their number transfers.

**It does not, and the reason is that this card spends its block elsewhere.**
`profile-af2-block.js`, all ten normalize labels summed:

| | block | normalize | share |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 400 residues, 512 sequences | 60.44 ms | 4.27 | **7.1%** |
| 825 residues, 512 sequences | 192.81 | 8.52 | **4.4%** |

The share FALLS with length here, where upstream's is 11.5% at 825, because the
attention and the contractions grow faster than the rows do: at 825 the four
flash kernels alone are 31% of the block.

`tools/gpu/bench-row-normalize.js` is the arrangement bench - five arms
interleaved, minimum of three rounds, 32 dispatches inside one pass because a
single one of these is shorter than the submit round trip and every arm
measured 2.3-2.4 ms of `onSubmittedWorkDone` before that was fixed:

| rows x channels | tree | subgroup1 | rows4 | rows8 | rows16 | rows8 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 204,800 x 256 (msa, 400 res) | 0.447 | 0.509 | 0.412 | **0.381** | 0.378 | 1.17x |
| 160,000 x 128 (pair, 400 res) | 0.253 | 0.206 | 0.200 | **0.172** | 0.175 | 1.47x |
| 29,972 x 256 | 0.106 | 0.109 | 0.075 | **0.075** | 0.075 | 1.41x |
| 60,416 x 64 | 0.106 | 0.075 | 0.075 | **0.075** | 0.075 | 1.41x |

🔴 **AND THE CONTROL IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** `subgroup1` is one row a
workgroup of 32 with `subgroupAdd` - the barrier-free reduction and NOT the rows
a workgroup covers. At 256 channels it is **SLOWER than the tree** (0.509
against 0.447, and 0.109 against 0.106), and it only wins where the row is
narrow enough that the tree had one channel a lane. So the reduction is not the
win on this card; the rows a workgroup covers is, exactly as upstream says, but
worth less here. `rows16` is `rows8` to the third digit.

1.17x-1.47x on 7.1% of a block is **about 1.3% of a block at 400 residues and
less at 825**, against rewriting four shipped kernels and adding a subgroup-width
dependency to each. Not taken. The bench stays, so the next person reads this
table instead of running the experiment again - and so that a device where the
normalize share IS 11% can ask the question in one command.

Two arrangements upstream measured and rejected, not re-run here because their
reasons are arithmetic rather than hardware: caching the row in workgroup memory
so the source is read once instead of three times (the re-reads already hit
cache), and taking both moments in one pass so the variance comes from
`E[x^2] - mean^2` (3-5% faster and four to twenty-five times less accurate).

## What the priors table is actually for, knob by knob

🔴 **IT WAS DOING TWO DIFFERENT JOBS AND ONLY ONE OF THEM IS A TABLE.**
`--no-prior=<knob>` restores one knob at a time from the prior, so a machine
that HAS a prior can price each one against what an unrecognised device gets.
Warm repeat of an AF2 fold at 512 clusters and 1024 extras, minimum of three:

| arm | ms | recovers |
|---|---:|---:|
| the full prior | 421 | - |
| no prior at all | 639 | - |
| ...keeping `matrixLinear` | 544 | **95 ms** |
| ...keeping `attentionMatrix` | 588 | 51 |
| ...keeping `attentionProjectMatrix` | 600 | 39 |
| ...keeping `attentionGroup` | 621 | 18 |
| ...keeping `opmMatrixContract` | 627 | 12 |
| ...keeping `stagedMatrixPrefetch` | 632 | 7 |
| ...keeping `linearTallTile` | 640 | 0 |
| ...keeping `triangleProjectMatrix` | 639 | 0 |
| ...keeping `opmProjectOutputPairs` | 639 | 0 |

They are close to additive and they account for the whole 218 ms. **Every
significant one asks the same question - does this device have subgroup matrix
units, and can this kernel feed them - and the API ANSWERS that.** It is not an
architecture secret and it never needed a table.

So `matrixCapabilityTuning` in src/runtime/device-profile.js sets five knobs
from `chromium-experimental-subgroup-matrix` + `shader-f16` + an f16
configuration, underneath the priors so a measured architecture still wins.
On this card, with the prior removed:

| arm | ms |
|---|---:|
| the prior | 416 |
| **no prior, capability only** | **406** |
| neither (`--default-tuning`) | 643 |

**1.58x for a GPU nobody has measured**, from five booleans the adapter already
told us. Nothing changes on the two machines that have priors - AF2's checksum
is -1725774 either way.

🔴 **AND AF3's GAP IS NOT LIKE THAT AT ALL, WHICH IS WHY THIS STOPS AT FIVE
KNOBS.** The same sweep on `fold.js --folds=2`, where `sample-start` is 1867 ms
with the prior and 4517 without:

| keeping | sample-start | recovers |
|---|---:|---:|
| `diffusionSplitK` | 2725 | **1792 ms** |
| `diffusionTokenTile` | 3414 | 1103 |
| `diffusionBatchedGates` | 3817 | 700 |
| `diffusionNormSplit` | 3991 | 526 |
| `atomRowTile` | 4201 | 316 |
| `keepSamplerWeights` | 4322 | 195 |
| `diffusionAttendSubgroups` | 4530 | 0 |
| `keepTrunkWeights` | 4531 | 0 |
| `gridAttendMatrix`, `matrixLinear`, `pairTransitionSplit`, `triangleProjectMatrix`, `stagedMatrixPrefetch`, `gridProjectMatrix` | 4514-4551 | 0 |

Tiles and split counts, every one of them a measurement about how many
workgroups fill this card and how many registers a kernel may hold. No
capability states those, and deriving them from a device limit would be a table
again with worse provenance. **AF3's prior stays a prior, and that is where a
runtime calibration would have to go** - which is the shape of upstream's
`calibrateGemmVariant`, 662-764 ms of every cold start until they cached it.
The capability layer is worth 0 on AF3 (6014 ms against 6015) and worth nothing
measurable on an ESMFold2 fold at 59 residues (0.867 s against 0.868), and
neither is a disappointment: it is the layer doing only what it claims.

Not extended to `triangleProjectMatrix` even though it is a matrix knob: it
measured zero on AF2 here, it wants `triangleProjectMatrixMinChannels` beside
it, and a threshold is a geometry number. Untested, so unset.

## The number every diffusion geometry knob is really about

🔴 **AF3's PRIOR IS FIVE GEOMETRY KNOBS AND THEY ASK ONE QUESTION.**
`diffusionSplitK` splits a kernel's K to make more workgroups, because at a
small token count there are too few to fill the card; `crossover` turns it off
again past a few hundred tokens, where the kernel fills the card by itself;
`atomRowTile`'s note says the shipped 256 "picks a tile of four at 1632 atoms -
408 workgroups of 64 lanes, **12% of this device**". Every one of them needs the
same unknown: **how many workgroups does this device run at once.** A table
cannot know it for a GPU nobody has run.

`tools/gpu/probe-occupancy.js` measures it, in about a second. The kernel is a
long dependent chain in registers - fixed time per workgroup, no memory traffic
- so N workgroups take one workgroup's time until the device is full and grow
linearly after. On this A100:

```
    1  1.400      2..4542  2.300       6359  3.500
```

| | |
|---|---:|
| plateau edge, at a 1.35x margin | **4542 workgroups** |
| the same from the slope past saturation, `N * floor / time` | **4432** |
| lanes | **~290,000** |

The two estimates are independent and agree to 2.5%, which is what says the
plateau was read rather than invented. For scale, an A100 is 108 SMs at 2048
resident threads, 221,184 - and this kernel is register-light, so more of these
64-lane groups fit than that division suggests.

🔴 **AND IT CORROBORATES A NUMBER THAT WAS ESTIMATED BY HAND.** `atomRowTile`'s
note computes 408 workgroups as 12% of this device, i.e. about 3,400 - the same
quantity, within 1.3x, arrived at from SM counts rather than a stopwatch.
`transitionThreadTarget: 100000` is the same fact again, written as threads.

Two traps it walked into first, both recorded in the file:

- 🔴 **AT 4096 CHAIN STEPS EVERY COUNT BELOW 1024 MEASURED THE SAME 0.2-0.3 ms**,
  which is `onSubmittedWorkDone` and not the kernel, and the plateau edge was
  invisible. The chain is 65,536 steps now, so one workgroup is milliseconds and
  the round trip is a fifth of one.
- 🔴 **AND ONE WORKGROUP IS NOT THE FLOOR.** It measures 1.4 ms where two
  through four thousand all measure 2.3. A single workgroup is not a scaled-down
  device; taking it as the baseline puts the whole plateau at 1.64x "floor" and
  finds an edge everywhere. The floor comes from counts 2 through 8.

What this is FOR is the next step and is not done: the diffusion geometry is
still a prior, so an unmeasured GPU still loses AF3's 2650 ms. The design the
measurement points at is to express those knobs as a THREAD TARGET rather than a
split count - which `transitionThreadTarget` already is - and derive the split
at dispatch from the target and the workgroups the kernel is about to launch.
That is a refactor of every diffusion dispatch and it should be measured knob by
knob against the prior's numbers above, not landed on the strength of this
plateau alone.

## Deriving AF3's split from the measured width: 1716 ms of the prior's 1792

The section above measures this card at ~4500 workgroups and stops there,
saying the next step is to spend it. This is that step, for the one knob worth
most: `diffusionSplitK`, which `--no-prior=diffusionSplitK` prices at **1792 ms
of a 2655 ms sampler gap**.

🔴 **THE FIRST THING THE SWEEP SAID IS THAT `splits` NEED NOT BE DERIVED AT
ALL.** `fold.js --split-k=<json>` sets the whole rule, which no flag could
before - `splits` and `tile` had none, and the patch flags needed a rule to
already exist, so `--no-prior` could not reach any of them. Swept on an
unrecognised device with everything else held at the prior's values:

| splits | 4 | 8 | 16 | 32 | 48 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| sample-start | 2857 | **2754** | 2780 | 2816 | 2978 |

**8 to 32 is a plateau, within 2%**, and the prior's 16 sits in the middle of
it. So the number a table was holding was never the delicate part.

🔴 **WHAT IS NOT PORTABLE IS WHETHER TO SPLIT AT ALL.** The split exists because
a small token count leaves the device idle: the unsplit attention projection
launches `ceil(rows / tile) * (width / lanes)` workgroups, 51 of them at 68
tokens. On a card a fiftieth of this one's width those 51 already fill it, and
the partial traffic plus the reduce pass are pure cost. The prior spells that
boundary as `crossover: 512` **tokens**, which is this device's answer wearing a
portable-looking costume. `derivedSplitRule` asks the question the crossover is
a proxy for - is the unsplit dispatch already as wide as the device - and the
token count falls out of it.

`src/runtime/occupancy.js` is the measurement a fold can afford: six counts
rather than the tool's twenty-six, one warm-up and two rounds, about a fifth of
a second. `requestAlphaFoldDevice` starts it and does not await it, and ONLY
where the tuning has no diffusion rule - so the two machines with priors never
pay for it, and every consumer reads "not measured yet" as "use the default".

| AF3 sampler, `sample-start` | ms |
|---|---:|
| the prior | 1877 |
| no prior, before this | 4500-4533 |
| **no prior, derived** | **2784** |
| no prior, the prior's own rule forced by hand | 2732 |

**1716 ms of the 1792, within 2% of the hand-tuned rule, on a device the table
does not name.** The coarse probe reads 2048 where the fine one reads 4542,
which is the right answer to a coarser question: the split count only has to
land inside an eight-to-thirty-two plateau.

🔴 **AND ITS FIRST READING WAS 4 WORKGROUPS, ON A CARD THE TOOL MEASURES AT
4542.** One timing per count, with the pipeline's first dispatch among them:
this probe reads a RATIO between counts, so a slow first reading does not add
noise, it moves the floor and collapses the plateau. A warm-up dispatch and two
rounds of minimums fix it. A probe whose failure mode is a plausible small
number needs a second opinion, which is what `probe-occupancy.js` printing its
whole table is for.

What is NOT derived, and the code says so: the tiles and the other three
kernels' split counts are this A100's measured values, carried along because a
device that wants a K split at all wants the tile the split pays for. A second
measured device may want different ones. Deriving `splits` from a plateau and
labelling the rest as one card's numbers is the honest split of what was
measured from what was assumed.

### And the atom row tile, which was the same question with a 256 in it

With the K split derived, `--no-prior=<knob>` re-prices what is LEFT of the
2643 ms - and the order changes completely:

| keeping, on top of the derived baseline of 2858 | sample-start | recovers |
|---|---:|---:|
| `atomRowTile` | 2433 | **425 ms** |
| `diffusionBatchedGates` | 2575 | 283 |
| `diffusionTokenTile` | 2623 | 235 |
| `keepSamplerWeights` | 2688 | 170 |
| `transitionThreadTarget` | 2784 | 74 |

`outputRowTileFor(queryRows, target = 256)` takes the largest row tile that
still leaves `target` workgroups, and its own comment says the 256 "is a
workgroup target and it is an M2's" - the number this file has now measured.
Passing the measured width instead of the constant picks tile ONE at 1632 rows
on this card, which is exactly what the prior asks for below its crossover,
arrived at from the mechanism rather than from the table.

| AF3 sampler, `sample-start` | ms |
|---|---:|
| `--default-tuning`, neither layer | 4525 |
| derived K split only | 2784 |
| **plus the derived atom tile** | **2437** |
| the full hand-measured prior | 1882 |

**2088 ms of the 2643 gap, 79%, from two measured mechanisms.** What remains is
`diffusionBatchedGates` (a memory trade, not a geometry one - ~14 MB of
duplicated f16 weights), `diffusionTokenTile` and `keepSamplerWeights`, and they
are the next three to ask the same question of.

### And the batched zero gate, which is a budget question wearing geometry's coat

283 ms, and unlike the two above it is not about the device's width at all. The
gate is a pure win wherever its duplicate FITS - `~14 MiB` of concatenated f16
weights plus four scratch buffers over the blocks - and a refusal wherever it
does not, because under the budget fallback the blocks are uploaded and released
per call and a duplicate of weights that are NOT being kept is exactly the trade
that fallback exists to refuse. So `batchedGatesAffordable` asks the BUDGET.

The headroom demanded is three times the estimate rather than one, and the
estimate deliberately doubles the measured 14 MiB: this guard is taken before
the sampler's own scratch exists, and erring towards declining costs 283 ms
where erring the other way costs the fold.

| AF3 sampler, `sample-start` | ms |
|---|---:|
| `--default-tuning`, every layer off | 4501 |
| derived K split | 2784 |
| plus the derived atom tile | 2437 |
| **plus the derived batched gate** | **2252** |
| the full hand-measured prior | 1886 |

**2249 of the 2615, 86%.**

🔴 **AND A DERIVATION IS NOT A PRIOR, WHICH `--default-tuning` HAD TO LEARN.**
The budget rule reads no tuning at all, so stripping the prior and the
capability layer left it on - and the arm that prices DEFAULT_TUNING alone
silently changed from 4525 ms to 3765, measuring something that no longer had a
name. `deviceDerivationsAllowed` is the switch: `--no-prior` keeps the
derivations, because measuring is exactly what an unrecognised device does, and
`--default-tuning` suppresses them, which puts that arm back at 4501.

### The last two: the token tile is the same fill question, retention is the same budget one

`diffusionTokenTile`'s own note in device-profile.js already said what the rule
should be - *"the ceiling was never what bound this, occupancy was, which is
exactly why the answer inverts on a machine with ten times the cores. A smaller
tile is more workgroups."* The model's `min(4, fits(channels))` is that sentence
answered for a device with a handful of cores. `derivedTokenTile` answers it
with the measured width, and gives 1 at 68 tokens here - the prior's own value
below its crossover.

`keepTrunkWeights` and `keepSamplerWeights` are the batched gate again:
releasing them costs a LATER fold 561 MiB in 1341 writeBuffer calls and 325 MiB
in 24 int5 passes, waited for on the queue rather than in a compute pass, and
the only unknown is whether the card has room. `keepResidentAffordable` says
yes with no ceiling, and under one only where what is already resident is under
a third of it.

| AF3 sampler, `sample-start` | ms |
|---|---:|
| `--default-tuning`, every layer off | 4510 |
| derived K split | 2784 |
| plus the derived atom tile | 2437 |
| plus the derived batched gate | 2252 |
| plus the derived token tile | 2129 |
| **plus derived retention** | **1952** |
| the full hand-measured prior | 1874 |

**2558 of the 2636, 97%** - and the whole fold is 3398 ms against the prior's
3392, which is the same fold. A device nobody has measured now gets AF3's
geometry from five mechanisms: its width for the K split, the atom tile and the
token tile; its memory budget for the batched gate and for retention.

### Checking the derivations on hardware that does not exist here

🔴 **THEY ARE NOW THE DEFAULT FOR EVERY GPU NOBODY HAS RUN, AND THEY WERE
CHECKED ON ONE.** A fill rule that is right at 4542 workgroups can be wrong at
64, and the devices it would be wrong on are exactly the ones with no prior to
fall back to. `--occupancy=<n>` makes this machine answer as a device of any
width, so the CHOICE each rule makes can be swept even though the hardware
cannot be:

| answering as | sample-start | whole fold |
|---|---:|---:|
| `--default-tuning`, every layer off | 4544 | 6032 |
| 16 workgroups | 3578 | **5030** |
| 64 | 2834 | 4291 |
| 256 | 2281 | 3723 |
| 2048 | 2020 | 3446 |
| the real measurement | 1952 | 3365 |
| the hand-measured prior | 1888 | 3406 |

Monotone in width and every arm strictly better than the default, so no width
makes a fold slower than it is today.

🔴 **AND THE SIMULATION VALIDATES THE CHOICE, NOT THE OUTCOME.** The width-16
arm is an A100 running a narrow device's configuration; it says the choice is
not pathological and it cannot say what that configuration costs on the hardware
it is for. Which is why the atom tile's target is **clamped to never fall below
the shipped 256**: the mechanism only ever argues that a WIDE device is left
idle, so it may make the tile smaller and never larger. Unclamped, width 16
picks a tile of 8 against the shipped 4 and measures 4735 ms here against the
clamped 5030 - so the clamp costs about 300 ms on this hardware and buys "no
narrow device is worse than today" by construction, on hardware there is none of
to measure. Reversible with evidence; both numbers are here.

🔴 **AND ONE READING OF 7224 ms NEARLY BOUGHT A FIX FOR A BUG THAT WAS NOT
THERE.** The first width-16 sweep reported the whole fold WORSE than the
default - 7224 against 6056 - while its sampler improved, which reads exactly
like a rule misfiring outside the stage it governs. Repeated: 4735 and 4796
against 6071 and 6006. One run of a two-fold tool on a machine that drifts by up
to 3.2x is not a measurement, which is this file's own rule two sections up, and
it was nearly acted on.

🔴 **THE TWO RULES DISAGREE ABOVE THE CROSSOVER AND ONLY ONE OF THEM IS
MEASURED.** The prior switches to a tile of 8 past 3000 query rows; the fill
rule reaches 8 only at 16,384, because past the crossover the trade stops being
occupancy and becomes weight traffic. On this card the prior wins and overrides
the derivation, so the disagreement costs nothing here - but a device relying on
the fill rule at a large structure is getting a mechanism that was verified at
one end of its range and not the other. Say so before extending it.

## Auditing every knob for whether it does anything

Three dead knobs turned up in one day and all three by accident:
`matrixLinear: false` fell through into the matrix path because the gate tested
only null and undefined; `attentionProjectMatrix` was set from a feature list
that says the units exist without saying they are 8x8, and crashed AF2 and the
multimer outright on an M2; and `pairTransitionChunkBytes` sat in an options
object its only reader never looked at. Three by accident implies more by
searching.

`tools/audit-knobs.py` searches. For each knob it runs one workload with a value
different from the one this device resolves and compares two digests from
`probe-compiles.js`: every shader compiled, by label and content, and every
dispatch, by label and grid.

| workload | moved something | moved nothing | not expressible | run failed |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `fold-af2` | 7 | 33 | 9 | 2 |
| `fold` (AF3) | 13 | 27 | 9 | 10 |

🔴 **"MOVED NOTHING" IS SUSPICIOUS AND NOT DEAD, AND MOST OF IT IS NEITHER.** A
diffusion knob cannot move anything in an AF2 fold, and a knob can be alive and
still move neither digest - `keepTrunkWeights` decides whether buffers are
released, `batchComputePasses` decides how dispatches group into passes,
`deviceFeaturisationMinBytes` decides a host route. The tool says where to look.

What it found by looking:

🔴 **FOUR ENTRIES IN THE AMPERE PRIOR ARE INERT, SHADOWED BY THE MATRIX PATHS.**
`attentionGroup: 4`, `attentionVectorScore: true`, `linearTallTile: true` and
`opmProjectOutputPairs: 4` move neither digest in an AF2 fold, because
`attentionMatrix: true` and `matrixLinear: {}` select kernels that do not read
them. They are not wrong - each was measured, and each would matter with the
matrix paths off - but they are dead weight today, and `linearTallTile`'s own
entry records "1.551x, relRMS 0, a whole AF2 fold bit identical" from before
`matrixLinear` existed. A reader would reasonably think they are active.

🔴 **AND TWO KEYS DID NOT NAME THEIR SHADER**, both caught by the collision
check, both in arms nobody had run:

- `--tune=triangleProjectMatrix=false` killed AF3. The triangle's weights are
  packed INTERLEAVED where the matrix projection runs and separately where it
  does not, so the WGSL differs at `const W_LINEARABWEIGHT` against `const
  W_LINEARAPWEIGHT`, and the tri key named the direction, the precisions and the
  scratch storages but not the pack. `src/evoformer/block.js` learned this at the
  same seam and puts its project tile in the key; the AF3 track never did.
- `--tune=singleProjectWorkgroupTarget=220` (and 55) killed it too.
  `projectSplits` is derived from that knob, baked into the source AND multiplied
  into the dispatch, and the key named the precision and both lane counts and not
  the split.

Both are now in their keys, both arms fold, and the collision error names the
first differing line rather than only the key - which is what turned the second
one from an afternoon into a minute.

🔴 **AND NINE KNOBS THE TOOL COULD NOT REACH - WHICH IS NOW ZERO.** `--tune`
splits its argument on commas, so an object-valued knob could not be written on
a command line at all, and a tile-valued one could be written but not GUESSED.
`--tune-json={"matrixLinear":false}` in gpu-chrome.mjs takes a whole patch and
parses it, and the audit carries a table of second values for the tile knobs.
With the gap closed the AF2 audit is **11 moved, 38 unmoved, 0 unreachable** -
`attentionMatrixTile`, `halfPrecision`, `matrixLinear` and `stagedMatrixResult`
all move a shader, and none of the four had ever been in an arm.

🔴 **AND CLOSING IT FOUND THE SAME BUG A THIRD TIME.**
`--tune-json={"trianglePairProjectTile":false}` killed AF2 with "projectTile
undefinedxundefined is not a multiple of the 8x8 workgroup". `?? undefined` does
not treat `false` as off, so a boolean reached a tile resolver - the identical
shape to `matrixLinear: false` falling into the matrix path, in a different
file, found the same week. A grep for the pattern turns up **fifteen call
sites**.

`shapedKnob` is the fix and the distinction it draws is the point: a knob whose
value is a tile, an object or a count has no meaningful `false`, so `false`
reads as "nobody set one"; a BOOLEAN knob is the opposite - `attentionMatrix:
false` means do not use the matrix kernel - and must never go through it.
Applied to the thirteen shaped sites and to none of the boolean ones. All four
of `trianglePairProjectTile`, `atomRowTile`, `opmProjectOutputPairs` and
`diffusionGateTile` now fold with `false` where each used to fail or misbehave.

## The tools this file added

| Question | Tool |
|---|---|
| Is `probe-alu.js`'s vector ceiling real, or dead lanes? | `tools/gpu/probe-alu-lanes.js` |
| What does the host-device bus cost, each way? | `tools/gpu/probe-bus.js` |
| What is `grid.attend` alone, without the copies? | `tools/gpu/bench-grid-attend-passes.js` |
| Which matrix shapes does this device offer, and what do the type parameters mean? | `tools/gpu/check-subgroup-matrix-shapes.js` |
| Does the staged matrix projection compute the projection, in every precision? | `tools/gpu/check-staged-matrix.js` |
| Where does the staged matrix GEMM's time go? | `tools/gpu/probe-staged-gemm-parts.js` |
| What do the matrix units ISSUE at? | `tools/gpu/probe-matrix-ceiling.js` |
| Does `grid.attend` on the matrix units compute `grid.attend`, at every head width? | `tools/gpu/check-grid-attend-matrix.js` |
| Does a REAL bundle decode the same on the device as on the host? | `tools/gpu/check-bundle-device-decode.js` - check-quantised-upload.js writes its own shard, so it cannot see a record the planner reads differently |
| Does OpenDDE's confidence head still compute its PAE and PDE? | `tools/gpu/check-opendde-confidence.js` - the head's ONLY gate; a fold's geometry check cannot see a confidence number |
| Does the matrix triangle projection compute the vector one's answer? | `tools/gpu/check-triangle-project-matrix.js` - and the interleaved pack is inside the comparison, because an interleave off by a role returns a plausible tensor |
| Is a transition worth splitting off its fused kernel at THIS width? | `bench-transition.js --channels=` against `bench-evoformer-linear.js --shape=ef2wide,ef2down,af3wide,af3down` |
| ...and which geometry does it want, raced against the scalar kernel? | `bench-grid-attend-passes.js --arms=scalar,4x32,2x16` |
| ...and what does it cost INSIDE the trunk, which is the only legible place? | `bench-trunk.js --profile --tune=gridAttendMatrix=true` |
| **Can a machine with one width check what another would get?** | `--occupancy=<n>` on any GPU tool, with `--no-prior`. It answers as a device of that width, which is the only way to sweep a derivation that is a default for hardware nobody here has |
| **How many workgroups does this device run at once?** | `tools/gpu/probe-occupancy.js` - 4542 here by the plateau edge and 4432 by the slope, ~290,000 lanes. It is the unknown behind `diffusionSplitK`, `atomRowTile` and `transitionThreadTarget` alike |
| Is the row LayerNorm's arrangement worth changing on THIS device? | `tools/gpu/bench-row-normalize.js` - tree against one row a subgroup and four, eight and sixteen rows a workgroup. 1.17x-1.47x here, on 4.4-7.1% of a block; see the section above for why that is not the 3.6% of a trunk upstream measures |
| Does the triangle multiplication agree with its CPU reference at every WIDTH? | `tools/gpu/check-triangle-shapes.js` - it found every odd `cZ` wrong |
